People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Even if I look like I’m sitting calmly, inside it feels like this pressure to move or switch to something else. Sometimes I need to do something with my hands or I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At work especially, I’ll answer teammates before they’ve even finished explaining a bug or a question. Sometimes I’m already talking and then I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s like a recurring thought I can’t get rid of. It’s more like my mind jumps around than one thought repeating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily tasks or keeping track of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I have a hard time keeping everything in one place. Like my work tickets, notes about stuff I need to fix at home, budget reminders—they end up scattered across Slack, sticky notes, email, and my phone. Then I waste time just figuring out what I was supposed to be doing instead of actually doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. In meetings I’m usually bouncing my knee or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair. I don’t always realize I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I’m like, okay, I should probably stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has a bit. At home and at work, people have had to follow up with me more than they used to, and I can tell it’s causing some friction. I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the person people don’t trust to handle planning or keep things moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I notice distractions sometimes, like anyone would, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether it’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever said something in a conversation that you later regretted because it was inappropriate or hurtful?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t think of a clear example where I said something hurtful and regretted it afterward. I mean, maybe I’ve been awkward or blunt sometimes, but nothing specific is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to finish tasks because you lose interest or get distracted before they're complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. It’s more that I start something and get partway through, then the last piece just doesn’t happen. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation, I’ll usually do the call or upload the form, but then the email sits there unfinished until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. In elementary school, I remember teachers moving my desk or telling me to stay seated because I’d keep popping up. I’d also talk before I was called on, or rush through assignments even when I understood them. So yeah, compared to other kids, I was probably more restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity your whole life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s been there since I was a kid, not something that started recently. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess what finally pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during things where I know I’m supposed to stay put. It’s been happening enough that it’s starting to bother me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uncomfortable when you have the urge to get up and leave these situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, uncomfortable more than anxious. Even if I look like I’m sitting calmly, inside it feels like this pressure to move or switch to something else. Sometimes I need to do something with my hands or I feel like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. At work especially, I’ll answer teammates before they’ve even finished explaining a bug or a question. Sometimes I’m already talking and then I realize I guessed wrong about where they were going, which is embarrassing.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I can get stuck thinking about work stuff sometimes, but I wouldn’t say it’s like a recurring thought I can’t get rid of. It’s more like my mind jumps around than one thought repeating.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your daily tasks or keeping track of time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I have a hard time keeping everything in one place. Like my work tickets, notes about stuff I need to fix at home, budget reminders—they end up scattered across Slack, sticky notes, email, and my phone. Then I waste time just figuring out what I was supposed to be doing instead of actually doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, pretty often. In meetings I’m usually bouncing my knee or clicking a pen, or just shifting around in my chair. I don’t always realize I’m doing it until someone glances over, and then I’m like, okay, I should probably stop.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms caused problems in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it has a bit. At home and at work, people have had to follow up with me more than they used to, and I can tell it’s causing some friction. I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the person people don’t trust to handle planning or keep things moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I notice distractions sometimes, like anyone would, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether it’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever said something in a conversation that you later regretted because it was inappropriate or hurtful?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t think of a clear example where I said something hurtful and regretted it afterward. It’s more that I start something and get partway through, then the last piece just doesn’t happen. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation, I’ll usually do the call or upload the form, but then the email sits there unfinished until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. In elementary school, I remember teachers moving my desk or telling me to stay seated because I’d keep popping up. I’d also talk before I was called on, or rush through assignments even when I understood them. So yeah, compared to other kids, I was probably more restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity your whole life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s been there since I was a kid, not something that started recently. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or remind me to stay seated because I kept popping up. I’d also talk before being called on, or rush through things even when I understood the work. So yeah, it seems pretty longstanding.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is with paying attention to details or with sitting still and being patient?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more with sitting still and being patient. Like, what finally made me come in is that I keep getting up during sprint planning or long mortgage calls, even when I know I really should just stay put. I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll go check something in the other room. It’s not always about missing details as much as feeling like I can’t just remain seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or make careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen sometimes. It’ll sit there until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to not being able to pay attention or to being overly active and impulsive?\n\nPatient: It feels more like being overly active or impulsive. The thing that really pushed me to come in was that I keep getting up during sprint planning or long mortgage calls even when I know I should stay put. I’ll suddenly feel like I need water, or I’ll decide to check something in the other room. It’s that restless “I can’t just sit here” feeling more than just not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when there are a few steps. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation, I’ll usually get through the first part, maybe the upload, and then somehow that last step just stays unfinished until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more because you can't focus on what you're doing or because you feel an internal restlessness and urge to be active?\n\nPatient: It’s more the internal restlessness, I think. Even if I’m sitting still and look calm, inside I feel this pressure to move or switch to something else, or at least do something with my hands. If I don’t, I start feeling really uncomfortable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly strained your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not like I’m having huge fights with people, but it has started creating friction at home and at work. My husband and coworkers have to follow up with me more than they used to, and I feel bad about that. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning or follow-through tasks, even though I’m genuinely trying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. At home and at work, people have had to follow up with me more than they used to, and I can tell it’s causing some friction. I’m trying hard, but I worry I’m becoming the person people don’t trust to handle planning or keep things moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I notice distractions sometimes, like anyone would, but I haven’t paid close attention to whether it’s a major issue for me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever said something in a conversation that you later regretted because it was inappropriate or hurtful?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t think of a clear example where I said something hurtful and regretted it afterward. I mean, maybe I’ve been awkward or blunt sometimes, but nothing specific is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to finish tasks because you lose interest or get distracted before they're complete?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes. It’s more that I start something and get partway through, then the last piece just doesn’t happen. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation, I’ll usually do the call or upload the form, but then the email sits there unfinished until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Were you more physically active or restless than your peers when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. In elementary school, I remember teachers moving my desk or telling me to stay seated because I’d keep popping up. I’d also talk before I was called on, or rush through assignments even when I understood them. So yeah, compared to other kids, I was probably more restless.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of restlessness and impulsivity your whole life, or did they start at some point?\n\nPatient: It feels like it’s been there since I was a kid, not something that started recently. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or remind me to stay seated because I kept popping up. I’d also talk before being called on, or rush through things even when I understood the work. So yeah, it seems pretty longstanding.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is with paying attention to details or with sitting still and being patient?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more with sitting still and being patient. Like, what finally made me come in is that I keep getting up during sprint planning or long mortgage calls, even when I know I really should just stay put. I’ll suddenly decide I need water, or I’ll go check something in the other room. It’s not always about missing details as much as feeling like I can’t just remain seated.\n\nDoctor: Do you often struggle to follow through on instructions or finish tasks because you lose focus or make careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that does happen sometimes. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation, I’ll usually do the first part, maybe even the upload, and then the last step just… doesn’t happen. It’ll sit there until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to not being able to pay attention or to being overly active and impulsive?\n\nPatient: It feels more like being overly active or impulsive. The thing that really pushed me to come in was that I keep getting up during sprint planning or long mortgage calls even when I know I should stay put. I’ll suddenly feel like I need water, or I’ll decide to check something in the other room. It’s that restless “I can’t just sit here” feeling more than just not paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on tasks that require sustained mental effort?\n\nPatient: Yes, especially when there are a few steps. Like if my husband asks me to call the insurance company, upload a form, and then email the confirmation, I’ll usually get through the first part, maybe the upload, and then somehow that last step just stays unfinished until he reminds me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more because you can't focus on what you're doing or because you feel an internal restlessness and urge to be active?\n\nPatient: It’s more the internal restlessness, I think. Even if I’m sitting still and look calm, inside I feel this pressure to move or switch to something else, or at least do something with my hands. If I don’t, I start feeling really uncomfortable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly strained your relationships with family or friends?\n\nPatient: Yes, somewhat. It’s not like I’m having huge fights with people, but it has started creating friction at home and at work. My husband and coworkers have to follow up with me more than they used to, and I feel bad about that. I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning or follow-through tasks, even though I’m genuinely trying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main problem is with staying focused on tasks or with controlling your impulses and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the restlessness and impulse side. I can be sitting there looking fine, but inside I feel this pressure to move or switch to something else. If I don’t, I get really uncomfortable, like I need to do something with my hands or I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
